<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:gw-constant"></a>
For a graph with nonnegative edge weights, Max-Cut asks for a partition of the vertices maximizing the weight of edges crossing the partition. A cut is encoded by signs on vertices, and an edge contributes exactly when its signs differ. For a maximization problem, an $\alpha$-approximation returns a feasible solution worth at least $\alpha$ times the optimum. Unless stated otherwise, the graph and its rational weights are given explicitly. We write $\operatorname{MaxCut}(G)$ for the maximum crossing weight and, when the edge weights sum to one, also denote this value by $\mathop{\mathrm{Val}}(G)$. The decision problem of whether a weighted graph has a cut of at least a given weight appears among Karp’s original NP-complete problems (Karp 1972) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Karp72](#ref-Karp72)]. Approximation asks how much of this optimum can still be guaranteed in polynomial time. Goemans and Williamson (Goemans and Williamson 1995) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[GW95](#ref-GW95)] gave a semidefinite programming algorithm with random-hyperplane rounding for Max-Cut, with expected approximation ratio approaching $$\label{eq:gw-constant}
 \alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}=\min_{-1\le \rho<1}
 \frac{2\arccos\rho}{\pi(1-\rho)}
 =0.878567\ldots.$$ The expression compares two contributions of an edge whose endpoint vectors have inner product $\rho$: its semidefinite objective value is $(1-\rho)/2$, whereas a random hyperplane separates the vectors with probability $\arccos(\rho)/\pi$. The minimum ratio over $\rho$ is the uniform guarantee of this rounding rule. Determining whether this ratio is optimal under ordinary NP-hardness has been a central question in approximation algorithms. Håstad’s hardness theorem (Håstad 2001) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Hastad01](#ref-Hastad01)], combined with the gadgets of Trevisan, Sorkin, Sudan, and Williamson (Trevisan et al. 2000) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[TSSW00](#ref-TSSW00)], rules out ratios greater than $16/17$. Khot, Kindler, Mossel, and O’Donnell (Khot et al. 2007) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[KKMO07](#ref-KKMO07)] established optimality of $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}$ assuming the Unique Games Conjecture (Khot 2002) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[Khot02](#ref-Khot02)]; the analytic input is the Majority Is Stablest theorem of Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz (Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz 2010) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[MOO10](#ref-MOO10)].

We resolve the unconditional optimality question for Max-Cut.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *For every fixed $\alpha$ with $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}<\alpha\le1$, it is NP-hard to approximate Max-Cut within a factor of $\alpha$. This holds for simple unweighted graphs.*

</div>

The statement means that a polynomial-time algorithm which always returns a cut of weight at least $\alpha$ times the maximum would imply $\mathrm P=\mathrm{NP}$. We prove the following more precise gap theorem. Write <a id="eq:B"></a>$$\label{eq:B}
 B(t)=\frac{2}{\pi}\arcsin t,\qquad 0<t<1.$$

<a id="stmt-thm-gap"></a>
<div id="thm:gap" class="theorem">

**Theorem 2**. *Fix a rational $t\in(0,1)$ and a constant $0<\varepsilon<(t-B(t))/4$. For graphs equipped with a rational probability distribution on edges, it is NP-hard to distinguish $$\begin{aligned}
 \textnormal{YES:}\quad&\mathop{\mathrm{Val}}\ge\frac{1+t}{2}-\varepsilon,\\
 \textnormal{NO:}\quad&\mathop{\mathrm{Val}}\le\frac{1+B(t)}{2}+\varepsilon,
\end{aligned}$$ where $\mathop{\mathrm{Val}}$ is the maximum cut weight.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Prior work and the direct route

The reduction of proof verification to approximation gaps has its roots in Feige, Goldwasser, Lovász, Safra, and Szegedy (Feige et al. 1996) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[FGLSS96](#ref-FGLSS96)] and in the PCP theorems of Arora–Safra and Arora, Lund, Motwani, Sudan, and Szegedy (Arora and Safra 1998; Arora et al. 1998) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[AroraSafra98](#ref-AroraSafra98), [ALMSS98](#ref-ALMSS98)]. Håstad’s Fourier analysis of proof-verification tests (Håstad 2001) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[Hastad01](#ref-Hastad01)] made many of these gaps sharp. For Max-Cut, the Gaussian geometry of the semidefinite relaxation gives a particularly precise target. Feige and Schechtman (Feige and Schechtman 2002) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[FS02](#ref-FS02)] constructed integrality gaps approaching $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}$ for this relaxation; a limitation of that relaxation alone does not establish hardness for arbitrary algorithms. The Unique Games reduction of Khot, Kindler, Mossel, and O’Donnell (Khot et al. 2007) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[KKMO07](#ref-KKMO07)] connected the same geometric threshold to computational hardness.

The original analysis of Khot, Kindler, Mossel, and O’Donnell proposed Majority Is Stablest as the analytic statement needed to obtain this threshold. Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz proved it through a comparison between low-influence Boolean functions and Gaussian functions (Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz 2010) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[MOO10](#ref-MOO10)], transferring Borell’s halfspace noise-stability bound (Borell 1985) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Borell85](#ref-Borell85)] to the Boolean setting. Their theorem leaves the Unique Games Conjecture as the hypothesis in the classical hardness reduction (Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz 2010, Corollary 2.12) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[MOO10](#ref-MOO10)]. We use Majority Is Stablest to identify an influential coordinate of our test, then prove the additional extraction needed to turn that coordinate into a source-game label. The algorithmic benchmark above is the ideal rounding ratio; finite-precision SDP optimization and random-hyperplane rounding give expected ratio $\alpha_{\mathrm{GW}}-\eta$ for any fixed $\eta>0$ (Goemans and Williamson 1995, sec. 2) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[GW95](#ref-GW95)].

The comparison between semidefinite approximation and hardness extends beyond a single worst-case ratio. O’Donnell and Wu (O’Donnell and Wu 2008) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[OW08](#ref-OW08)] determined the full Max-Cut approximation curve under the Unique Games Conjecture, relating the attainable cut value to the instance’s optimum. Raghavendra (Raghavendra 2008) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Raghavendra08](#ref-Raghavendra08)] established a general correspondence between semidefinite relaxations and Unique-Games-based hardness for finite constraint satisfaction problems. Our theorem concerns the unconditional worst-case ratio for Max-Cut; the proof below establishes the specific family of completeness and soundness gaps in [thm:gap](#thm:gap).

In a projection game, each vertex of a bipartite graph receives a label from a specified alphabet, and the edge weights form a probability distribution. An edge accepts when its prescribed map sends the left label to the right label. Its value is the maximum accepted edge weight. A two-to-one constraint has exactly two preimages for every right label. Completeness and soundness are, respectively, the promised lower bound on the YES-case value and upper bound on the NO-case value.

Our starting point is the published 2-to-1 game construction with imperfect completeness (Dinur et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[DKKMS25](#ref-DKKMS25)]. The Grassmann-graph approach was introduced by Khot, Minzer, and Safra (Khot, Minzer, and Safra 2025) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[KMS25](#ref-KMS25)]. Its soundness was completed through the connection between expansion and agreement established by Barak, Kothari, and Steurer (Barak, Kothari, and Steurer 2019) <a id="pm-cite-21"></a>[[BKS19](#ref-BKS19)] and the expansion theorem of Khot, Minzer, and Safra (Khot, Minzer, and Safra 2023) <a id="pm-cite-22"></a>[[KMS23](#ref-KMS23)]. We need a precise affine form of the construction: its projections are surjective affine maps between binary spaces of dimensions differing by one, and its alphabet can be fixed for a desired soundness before the completeness error is made arbitrarily small. [prop:affine-games](#prop:affine-games) states this input. Appendix <a href="#sec:affine-games" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:affine-games">10</a> derives it from the published construction, specifying both the valid-subspace conditioning and the auxiliary candidate lists used in the soundness proof. Together with Majority Is Stablest, that construction supplies the external foundation of the argument. The Unique Games Conjecture enters only as historical context. The tree Fourier analysis, hashing bound, and hidden-coordinate decoding are proved here.

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### The proof and its main ingredients

As in long-code reductions (Bellare, Goldreich, and Sudan 1998; Håstad 2001) <a id="pm-cite-23"></a>[[BGS98](#ref-BGS98), [Hastad01](#ref-Hastad01)], a label $a$ is represented by the dictator $w\mapsto w(a)$, where $w$ ranges over Boolean words indexed by labels. The graph has one such word domain for each tuple of left vertices of the source game. Thus an arbitrary cut specifies a Boolean function on each domain. To sample an edge, the test chooses two left tuples through a common right tuple, generates two words on the right label space, and pulls them back through the sampled affine projections. It negates the second word. When the sampled constraints are satisfied, dictator labels give a cut probability of one half of one plus the correlation between the two words evaluated at a common label.

The word generator is a finite rooted tree. Every internal node has $s$ pairs of children and a random table from $\mathbb F_2^s$ to $\{-1,1\}$; the leaves carry affine forms on the label space. At a label $a$, these forms give leaf signs, and the tables recursively combine the child signs into the root sign $W(a)$. The gate rule is chosen so that each monomial of its Walsh expansion keeps one child from every pair. A second exploration keeps both children of one randomly selected pair at each reached node. At any fixed depth, the two explorations have exactly one node in common. The test couples the tables reached by this second exploration at a random depth, using entrywise correlation $t$, and leaves all other tables unchanged. Every monomial therefore acquires precisely one factor of $t$. A small independent resampling of the affine rows gives the smoothing needed for soundness and only a small completeness loss.

For an arbitrary cut, we first average its odd part over the source-game edges. The resulting function is balanced in the selected table entries, and a cut above the desired NO threshold makes its expected noise stability exceed $B(t)$. Majority Is Stablest then detects an influential entry. That entry is indexed by a gate input, not by a source-game label. The remaining task is to connect its influence to labels that the two endpoints of a source edge can choose consistently. Three steps address this task.

1.  *Local simplification of Fourier support.* The gate symmetries force the two child indices in each pair to have the same sum. After row smoothing, averaging the depth of the selected tables makes it inexpensive to retain indices which put this sum in one child of each pair and zero in the other. This simplification is imposed only at the influential gate. It is the structure that lets affine shifts act as translations of that gate’s table.

2.  *A vector-valued affine hashing lemma.* For independent affine shifts, a diffuse distribution of Fourier mass cannot create a large entry influence on average. The bound is uniform in the dimension of the row space. Its proof uses two truncations and a fourth-moment identity; it may be useful whenever translated coefficient arrays must be decoded without an alphabet-size loss.

3.  *Decoding with a hidden coordinate.* The mass concentration is obtained after fixing many row coordinates. A left endpoint cannot usually construct the pullback of this context without knowing its incident projection. We use a product of the game and plant zero linear coefficients in one uniformly chosen factor of the context and of the latent resampling rows. After that factor is forgotten, the planted law is close to the original law. At the planted factor, the context can be pulled back without knowing the projection. Only the $2s$ free rows at the selected gate still have to be compared across it, at a cost of $2^{2s}$ in Fourier mass.

The resulting decoding loss is independent of the source alphabet. This is essential: the alphabet dimension depends on the soundness we request, so a dimension-dependent loss could make the soundness choice circular. We first fix that loss and the source soundness, then the alphabet and the number of product factors, and finally the source completeness error. The quantifier order in [prop:affine-games](#prop:affine-games) permits exactly this sequence.

fixes the Fourier conventions. [sec:tree](#sec:tree), [sec:reduction](#sec:reduction) define the code and the Max-Cut test. [sec:tree-fourier](#sec:tree-fourier), [sec:hashing](#sec:hashing), [sec:extraction](#sec:extraction) prove the analytic ingredients. [sec:decoding](#sec:decoding), [sec:completion](#sec:completion) give the decoding and finish the proof. Appendix <a href="#sec:affine-games" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:affine-games">10</a> derives the precise starting game from the published construction, and Appendix <a href="#sec:unweighted" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:unweighted">11</a> gives a deterministic conversion to simple unweighted graphs.

<a id="sec:preliminaries"></a>
### Preliminaries

The proof uses two Fourier expansions. Characters of affine rows encode candidate labels, whereas characters of Boolean table entries detect the influences to which Majority Is Stablest applies. We introduce both conventions, then state the exact affine-game input.

All vector spaces over $\mathbb F_2$ are finite dimensional. All expectations on a finite vector space or Boolean cube use the uniform probability measure, unless another distribution is specified. All Fourier coefficients use this probability normalization. An $L^2$ norm always integrates over the variables displayed in its subscript; variables declared fixed are not integrated.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
#### Affine rows and labels

<a id="eq:labels-dual"></a>
Let $\mathcal A$ be a nonempty affine space over $\mathbb F_2$, with a fixed affine coordinate system, and set $$H=\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(\mathcal A,\mathbb F_2).$$ This is a vector space, containing the constant form $\mathbf 1$. For $q\in H^*$ define $\chi_q(h)=(-1)^{q(h)}$. Evaluation identifies $\mathcal A$ with $$\label{eq:labels-dual}
 \{q\in H^*:q(\mathbf 1)=1\}.$$ Indeed, if $h(a)=h_0+\sum_i h_i a_i$, a functional taking value one on $\mathbf 1$ has the unique form $q(h)=h_0+\sum_i h_i a_i$. We call precisely these functionals *labels*; a general nonzero functional in $H^*$ need not be a label.

For $f:H^k\to\mathbb R$ and $C=(q_1,\ldots,q_k)\in(H^*)^k$, put $$\widehat f(C)=\mathbb E_R f(R)\prod_{i=1}^k\chi_{q_i}(R_i),\qquad
 |C|=\#\{i:q_i\ne0\}.$$ Parseval gives $\sum_C\widehat f(C)^2=\mathbb E_R f(R)^2$. The same notation is used for functions with additional variables, which remain fixed during the indicated Fourier expansion.

For $0\le r\le1$, let $T_r$ be the row-noise operator: independently in each coordinate, retain the row with probability $r$ and otherwise replace it by an independent uniform row. Then <a id="eq:row-noise"></a>$$\label{eq:row-noise}
 \widehat{T_rf}(C)=r^{|C|}\widehat f(C).$$ This operator preserves the range $[-1,1]$. It is also convolution by an independent increment $E\in H^k$, whose coordinates are zero with probability $r$ and independent uniform rows with probability $1-r$. Convolution commutes with every fixed translation of the rows.

<a id="pm-h-7"></a>
#### Boolean Fourier analysis

<a id="eq:degree-influence"></a>
For a finite set $\Omega$ and $h:\{-1,1\}^\Omega\to\mathbb R$, write $$h(x)=\sum_{S\subseteq\Omega}\widehat h(S)\prod_{a\in S}x_a.$$ Its influence and degree-$K$ influence at $a\in\Omega$ are $$\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_a(h)=\sum_{S\ni a}\widehat h(S)^2,\qquad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_a^{\le K}(h)=\sum_{\substack{S\ni a\\|S|\le K}}
 \widehat h(S)^2.$$ Let $P_{\le K}$ be the orthogonal projection onto Fourier degree at most $K$. In particular, $$\label{eq:degree-influence}
 \sum_{a\in\Omega}\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_a(P_{\le K}h)
 \le K\norm{h}_2^2.$$ For $t$-correlated uniform bits $x,y$, independently coupled in each coordinate, <a id="eq:stability"></a>$$\label{eq:stability}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(h)=\mathbb E[h(x)h(y)]
 =\sum_{S\subseteq\Omega}t^{|S|}\widehat h(S)^2.$$ Thus stability is nonnegative for $t\ge0$. If $h\in[-1,1]$, it is at most one.

<a id="stmt-lem-mis"></a>
<div id="lem:mis" class="lemma">

**Lemma 3** (Majority Is Stablest with a degree cutoff). *Fix $0<t<1$ and $\xi>0$. There are an integer $K\ge1$ and $\tau>0$, depending only on $t,\xi$, such that every $h:\{-1,1\}^\Omega\to[-1,1]$ with $\mathbb Eh=0$ satisfies $$\bigl(\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_a^{\le K}(h)<\tau\text{ for all }a\in\Omega\bigr)
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(h)\le B(t)+\xi.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-mis"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-mis</summary>

*Proof.* The untruncated assertion follows from Majority Is Stablest (Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz 2010, Theorem 4.4) <a id="pm-cite-24"></a>[[MOO10](#ref-MOO10)]. To match its $[0,1]$ convention, put $g=(h+1)/2$: then $\mathbb Eg=1/2$, each nonconstant Fourier coefficient is halved, and $\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(h)=4\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(g)-1$. The Gaussian halfspace value for $g$ is $1/4+\arcsin(t)/(2\pi)$, which becomes $B(t)$ in our normalization; see (Mossel, O’Donnell, and Oleszkiewicz 2010, Theorem 4.1 and Corollary 4.3) <a id="pm-cite-25"></a>[[MOO10](#ref-MOO10)] and (Borell 1985) <a id="pm-cite-26"></a>[[Borell85](#ref-Borell85)]. Here is the degree-cutoff reduction to the untruncated assertion. For a cube-noise parameter $u<1$, put $h_u=T_u h$, using ordinary bit noise in this paragraph. Range and mean are preserved. Uniformly over $h$ of norm at most one, $$0\le\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(h)-\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(h_u)
 \le\sup_{j\ge0}t^j(1-u^{2j})\longrightarrow0
 \quad(u\uparrow1).$$ The convergence follows by first choosing a finite degree cutoff to bound the tail $t^j$, and then taking $u$ close to one on the remaining degrees. Choose $u$ so that this error is at most $\xi/2$. Let $\delta>0$ be the untruncated influence threshold for error $\xi/2$. Choose $K$ with $u^{2(K+1)}\le\delta/2$, and take $\tau=\delta/2$. Then $$\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_a(h_u)
 \le\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_a^{\le K}(h)+u^{2(K+1)}\norm{h}_2^2
 <\delta.$$ Apply the untruncated theorem to $h_u$ and add the stability error. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Affine projection games

A weighted bipartite projection game consists of finite sets $X,Y$, a rational probability distribution $\lambda$ on a finite edge set $E$ with endpoint maps to $X$ and $Y$ (parallel edges are allowed), finite alphabets, and a projection $\pi_e$ for each edge. Its value is $$\mathop{\mathrm{Val}}(\mathcal G)=
 \max_{\ell_X,\ell_Y}\Pr_{e=(x,y)\sim\lambda}
 [\pi_e(\ell_X(x))=\ell_Y(y)].$$ In this paper the alphabets are $\mathbb F_2^{n+1}$ and $\mathbb F_2^n$, and every $\pi_e:\mathbb F_2^{n+1}\to\mathbb F_2^n$ is surjective affine. [prop:affine-games](#prop:affine-games) supplies NP-hard gaps with this structure. We discard endpoints with zero marginal probability whenever conditional edge distributions are used.

The starting theorem has the quantifier order $$\forall b\in(0,1)\quad \exists n=n(b)\ge1\quad
 \forall a\in(0,1-b).$$ The precise promise is given below; its derivation from the published construction is in [sec:affine-games](#sec:affine-games).

<a id="stmt-prop-affine-games"></a>
<div id="prop:affine-games" class="proposition">

**Proposition 4** (Affine 2-to-1 Games Theorem). *For every constant $b\in(0,1)$ there is an integer $n\geq 1$ such that, for every constant $a\in(0,1-b)$, the following promise problem is NP-hard. Its input is a weighted bipartite game with vertex sets $X,Y$, respective label sets $\mathbb F_2^{n+1},\mathbb F_2^n$, and a surjective affine map $$\pi_e:\mathbb F_2^{n+1}\longrightarrow\mathbb F_2^n$$ on each edge $e=(x,y)$. An edge is satisfied when $\pi_e(\lambda_X(x))=\lambda_Y(y)$. The promise cases are $$\mathrm{YES}:\quad \mathop{\mathrm{Val}}(\mathcal G)\geq 1-a,
  \qquad
  \mathrm{NO}:\quad \mathop{\mathrm{Val}}(\mathcal G)\leq b.$$ The integer $n$ depends on $b$ alone. For fixed $a,b$, the reduction is deterministic and has polynomial running time and output size.*

</div>

A weighted graph is likewise specified by a rational distribution on unordered edges, allowing loops. A cut assigns signs to its vertices and satisfies an edge when its signs differ. Its value is its satisfaction probability. Loops have zero cut weight. The conversion in [lem:unweighted](#lem:unweighted) keeps a common scale even when loops are deleted.

<a id="sec:tree"></a>
### A tree code with complementary traversals

We use the affine label space $\mathcal A$ and row space $H=\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(\mathcal A,\mathbb F_2)$ of [sec:preliminaries](#sec:preliminaries). The code below turns affine rows and Boolean gate tables into a word indexed by labels. Its two complementary traversals control the correlation seen by a cut that evaluates the word at a single label.

<a id="stmt-def-tree-code"></a>
<div id="def:tree-code" class="definition">

<a id="eq:gate-rule"></a>
**Definition 5** (Tree code). Fix integers $s\ge2$ and $m\ge1$, and put $$k=(2s)^m,\qquad d=s^m.$$ Let $\mathcal T$ be the complete rooted $2s$-ary tree of depth $m$. The children of each internal node $v$ are indexed by $(g,b)\in[s]\times\{0,1\}$. Its leaves are indexed by $[k]$. To each internal node assign a table $F_v:\mathbb F_2^s\to\{-1,1\}$, and to each leaf assign a row $R_i\in H$. The word $W(R,F)\in\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal A}$ is defined pointwise as follows. At $a\in\mathcal A$, leaf $i$ has sign $(-1)^{R_i(a)}$. An internal node with child signs $(Z_{g,b})$ outputs $$\left(\prod_{g=1}^s Z_{g,0}\right)F_v(z),
 \qquad (-1)^{z_g}=Z_{g,0}Z_{g,1}.
 \label{eq:gate-rule}$$ The root output is $W(R,F)(a)$.

</div>

We choose the tables to be noised by a second traversal of the tree. A *random slice* is obtained by first choosing $J_0$ uniformly from $\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$. Starting at the root, at each reached node of depth less than $J_0$ choose one of its $s$ child pairs uniformly and independently, and continue through both children of that pair. The set $\mathcal D$ of reached nodes at depth $J_0$ is the slice.

Conditionally on $J_0=j$, the slice has $2^j$ nodes, and every fixed node of depth $j$ belongs to it with probability $s^{-j}$. We write $F_{\rm out}=(F_u)_{u\notin\mathcal D}$ for the tables outside the slice.

The two traversals of the tree used below complement one another. The slice construction chooses one pair and keeps both children. The Walsh expansion of a gate keeps one child from every pair. Their intersection therefore follows a single path. Figure <a href="#fig:traversals" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:traversals">1</a> shows the local rule; iterating it gives one common node at every slice depth.

<figure id="fig:traversals">

<figcaption>Complementary choices at one gate, illustrated for $s = 3$. A monomial traversal (solid) selects one child from each pair; the slice exploration (dashed) selects both children of one pair. Exactly one child is shared, shown in purple. Only this local selection is depicted; the same rule is repeated above the chosen slice.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="stmt-lem-tree-correlation"></a>
<div id="lem:tree-correlation" class="lemma">

**Lemma 6** (Complementary traversal and correlation). *For every fixed collection of tables $F$, the root output as a multilinear polynomial in the $k$ leaf signs has only degree-$d$ monomials. Each such monomial chooses one child from every pair at every node that it reaches. Every resulting traversal meets every possible slice $\mathcal D$ at exactly one node. Consequently, negating all the tables on a slice negates $W$.*

<a id="eq:tree-correlation"></a>
*Let $0<t<1$ and $0<r<1$. Draw $F$ uniformly, and construct $F'$ by leaving the tables outside $\mathcal D$ unchanged and coupling each pair of corresponding table entries on $\mathcal D$ as uniform signs of correlation $t$, independently across entries. Independently draw $R\in H^k$ uniformly, and form $R^1,R^2$ by retaining each row of $R$ with probability $r$ and otherwise replacing it by an independent uniform row, independently in the two copies. Then, for every $a\in\mathcal A$ and every fixed slice, $$\mathbb E\bigl[W(R^1,F)(a)W(R^2,F')(a)\mid\mathcal D\bigr]
       =t r^{2d}.
 \label{eq:tree-correlation}$$ In particular, if $r=1-\mu/d$ with $0<\mu<1/2$, then $r^{2d}\ge1-2\mu$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-tree-correlation"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-tree-correlation</summary>

<a id="eq:tree-polynomial"></a>
*Proof.* For a fixed gate table, expand in its argument $z$: $F_v(z)=\sum_{I\subseteq[s]}\widehat F_v(I)(-1)^{\sum_{g\in I}z_g}$. Substituting into Equation <a href="#eq:gate-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gate-rule">[eq:gate-rule]</a>, the term indexed by $I$ is $$\widehat F_v(I)
 \prod_{g\notin I}Z_{g,0}\prod_{g\in I}Z_{g,1}.$$ Iterating this expansion gives $$W_F(x)=\sum_{P\in\mathscr P}a_P(F)\prod_{i\in P}x_i,
 \qquad |P|=d,
 \label{eq:tree-polynomial}$$ where $\mathscr P$ is the family of leaf sets produced by choosing one child per pair at each reached internal node. The leaf set determines all these choices: a selected child has a nonempty intersection with that leaf set, whereas an unselected child has none. Thus different traversals give different monomials, and $a_P(F)$ is the product of one Walsh coefficient of $F_v$ for each node reached by the traversal.

At the root both a monomial traversal and the slice exploration are present. At any common node above the slice depth, exactly one child is selected by both explorations. Induction gives exactly one common node at the slice depth. Hence each $a_P$ contains exactly one factor from a table on $\mathcal D$. Negating every such table negates each coefficient in Equation <a href="#eq:tree-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-polynomial">[eq:tree-polynomial]</a> and therefore negates the word. Moreover, each Walsh coefficient of a slice table satisfies $$\mathbb E[\widehat F'_v(I)\mid F_v]=t\widehat F_v(I).$$ Conditional independence between tables therefore gives $\mathbb E[a_P(F')\mid F,\mathcal D]=t a_P(F)$.

For fixed $a$, the signs $x_i^b=(-1)^{R_i^b(a)}$ are uniform, independent across $i$, and $\mathbb E[x_i^1x_i^2]=r^2$. Thus the product expectation of two monomials in Equation <a href="#eq:tree-polynomial" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-polynomial">[eq:tree-polynomial]</a> is zero unless their leaf sets agree, and is $r^{2d}$ when they agree. Since $W_F$ is Boolean, Parseval gives $\sum_P a_P(F)^2=1$. Averaging first over rows and then over $F'$ proves Equation <a href="#eq:tree-correlation" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:tree-correlation">[eq:tree-correlation]</a>. The last claim is Bernoulli’s inequality: $(1-\mu/d)^{2d}\ge1-2\mu$. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:function-of-code"></a>
The correlation identity describes a cut that evaluates the word at one label. To analyze arbitrary cuts, we also need functions that can depend on the entire word but do not see its generating rows or tables. We call $f$ a *function of the code* if $$f(R,F)=\phi(W(R,F))
 \label{eq:function-of-code}$$ for a fixed function $\phi$ on words. This class is closed under averaging over fixed choices of $\phi$, including choices that first pull a word back through a fixed affine map. For $r\in[0,1]$, let $T_r$ denote independent row resampling with retention probability $r$, and write $\bar f=T_rf$. The operator preserves bounds on the range of $f$. If $\phi$ is odd, Lemma <a href="#lem:tree-correlation" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:tree-correlation">6</a> shows that both $f$ and $\bar f$ are odd under simultaneous negation of the slice tables. In particular, they have mean zero in those table entries when all rows and outside tables are fixed.

At these fixed data, the remaining random input is the cube of $|\mathcal D|2^s$ independent entries, indexed by $(v,z)\in\mathcal D\times\mathbb F_2^s$. Fourier degree and influence on this cube refer to subsets of *entries*. This differs from the Walsh expansion of a fixed gate in its $s$-bit argument $z$ used in the correlation proof.

<a id="sec:reduction"></a>
### The Max-Cut reduction

Fix a rational $t\in(0,1)$, tree parameters $s,m$, a rational $0<\mu<1/2$, and $r=1-\mu/d$. The parameters will be chosen in [sec:completion](#sec:completion). Let $\mathcal G$ be an affine game as in [prop:affine-games](#prop:affine-games), with edge law $\lambda$ and alphabet dimensions $n+1,n$. Let $T\ge1$ be an integer. We use the product label spaces $$\mathcal B=(\mathbb F_2^{n+1})^T,\qquad
 \mathcal A=(\mathbb F_2^n)^T,$$ and the row space $H=\mathop{\mathrm{Aff}}(\mathcal A,\mathbb F_2)$.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
#### Vertices and edge distribution

For each $\underline x\in X^T$, make one graph vertex $(\underline x,w)$ for every word $w\in\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal B}$. This is the standard long-code word domain (Bellare, Goldreich, and Sudan 1998; Håstad 2001) <a id="pm-cite-27"></a>[[BGS98](#ref-BGS98), [Hastad01](#ref-Hastad01)]; the tree construction specifies how the test samples its queries. Thus an arbitrary cut is exactly a family of functions $$\sigma_{\underline x}:\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal B}\longrightarrow\{-1,1\}.$$ The functions receive only the resulting words; they do not receive the rows, gate tables, slice, or sampled game edges that produced them.

The graph’s edge distribution is the following test.

1.  Draw $\underline y\in Y^T$ from the product right-endpoint marginal of $\lambda$. Independently draw two edge tuples $\underline e,\underline e'$ from the product conditional edge law given $\underline y$. Write $\underline x,\underline x'$ for their left endpoints and $\pi_{\underline e},\pi_{\underline e'}:\mathcal B\to\mathcal A$ for their coordinatewise projections.

2.  Independently of all game data, draw a random slice $\mathcal D$. Draw $F,F'$ as in [lem:tree-correlation](#lem:tree-correlation): corresponding entries are independent $t$-correlated uniform pairs on $\mathcal D$, and identical uniform pairs off $\mathcal D$.

3.  Draw a uniform common row array $R\in H^k$. In each of two copies, independently retain each row with probability $r$ and otherwise resample it uniformly, obtaining $R^1,R^2$.

4.  Output the unordered edge whose endpoints are <a id="eq:test-queries"></a>$$\label{eq:test-queries}
     \bigl(\underline x,W(R^1,F)\circ\pi_{\underline e}\bigr),
     \qquad
     \bigl(\underline x',-W(R^2,F')\circ\pi_{\underline e'}\bigr).$$

All edge weights are the probabilities of these outcomes, aggregated over outcomes with the same unordered endpoints.

<a id="stmt-lem-completeness"></a>
<div id="lem:completeness" class="lemma">

**Lemma 7** (Completeness). *If $\mathop{\mathrm{Val}}(\mathcal G)\ge1-a$, the graph has a cut of value at least <a id="eq:completeness"></a>$$\label{eq:completeness}
 (1-2Ta)\frac{1+t r^{2d}}2
 \ge\frac{1+t}{2}-t\mu-2Ta.$$ The first lower bound is useful when $2Ta\le1$, which is the regime used below.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-completeness"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-completeness</summary>

*Proof.* Choose game labels $\ell_X,\ell_Y$ satisfying an edge with probability at least $1-a$. Label each word vertex by evaluation at the tuple of left labels: $$\sigma_{\underline x}(w)=
 w\bigl(\ell_X(x_1),\ldots,\ell_X(x_T)\bigr).$$ Each coordinate of either sampled edge tuple has the original edge law. A union bound shows that all $2T$ sampled constraints are satisfied except with probability at most $2Ta$. When all these constraints are satisfied, the two queried tuples project to the same label $l=(\ell_Y(y_1),\ldots,\ell_Y(y_T))\in\mathcal A$. The cut signs then have product $-W(R^1,F)(l)W(R^2,F')(l)$. The word-generation data are independent of the game edges, so [lem:tree-correlation](#lem:tree-correlation) gives conditional cut probability $(1+t r^{2d})/2$. On the remaining event the cut probability is nonnegative. This proves the first bound when $2Ta\le1$; if $2Ta>1$ that bound is negative and is automatic. Finally, $r^{2d}\ge1-2\mu$, and the loss due to the prefactor is at most $2Ta$, giving the second bound. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
#### A large cut produces excess stability

An arbitrary cut need not change sign when its queried word is negated. We separate its two parities because the negative second query makes odd dependence increase the cut value and even dependence decrease it. For each cut table define its even and odd parts by $$\sigma^{\mathrm{even}}(w)=\frac{\sigma(w)+\sigma(-w)}2,
 \qquad
 \sigma^o(w)=\frac{\sigma(w)-\sigma(-w)}2.$$ For an edge tuple and a right-endpoint tuple, set <a id="eq:edge-function"></a><a id="eq:average-function"></a>$$\begin{aligned}
 G_{\underline e}(R,F)
 &=\sigma^o_{\underline x(\underline e)}
       (W(R,F)\circ\pi_{\underline e}),\label{eq:edge-function}\\
 G_{\underline y}(R,F)
 &=\mathbb E_{\underline e\mid\underline y}G_{\underline e}(R,F),
 \qquad \bar G_{\underline y}=T_rG_{\underline y}.
 \label{eq:average-function}
\end{aligned}$$ Write $A_{\underline y}$ and $\bar A_{\underline y}=T_rA_{\underline y}$ for the analogous averages using $\sigma^{\mathrm{even}}$. These functions are bounded in absolute value by one. Moreover, $$G_{\underline y}(R,F)=\phi_{\underline y}(W(R,F)),\qquad
 \phi_{\underline y}(w)=\mathbb E_{\underline e\mid\underline y}
 \sigma^o_{\underline x(\underline e)}(w\circ\pi_{\underline e}),$$ where $\phi_{\underline y}$ is odd and depends on no slice or row data. Thus $\bar G_{\underline y}$ has mean zero in the slice entries for every fixed $\underline y,\mathcal D,R,F_{\mathrm{out}}$, by the observation following [lem:tree-correlation](#lem:tree-correlation). The later extraction argument, [prop:extraction](#prop:extraction), applies to precisely such bounded odd functions of the generated word.

<a id="stmt-lem-cut-stability"></a>
<div id="lem:cut-stability" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:cut-stability"></a>
<a id="eq:cut-upper-stability"></a>
**Lemma 8** (Stability identity). *For every cut of the constructed graph, $$\label{eq:cut-stability}
 \operatorname{cut}(\sigma)=\frac12+
 \frac12\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t^{F_{\mathcal D}}(\bar G_{\underline y})
 -\frac12\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t^{F_{\mathcal D}}(\bar A_{\underline y}),$$ where both expectations are over $\underline y,\mathcal D,R,F_{\mathrm{out}}$. In particular, $$\label{eq:cut-upper-stability}
 \operatorname{cut}(\sigma)\le\frac12+
 \frac12\mathbb E\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t^{F_{\mathcal D}}(\bar G_{\underline y}).$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-cut-stability"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-cut-stability</summary>

*Proof.* Condition on $\underline y,\mathcal D,R,F_{\mathrm{out}}$. The sampled edge tuples and the two row resamplings are independent between copies. After averaging over them, the expected first sign as a function of $F_{\mathcal D}$ is $\bar A_{\underline y}+\bar G_{\underline y}$. The minus sign in the second queried word makes the expected second sign $\bar A_{\underline y}-\bar G_{\underline y}$ at $F'_{\mathcal D}$. The two slice inputs are ordinary $t$-correlated uniform cube inputs. The rows in these functions are the common latent array $R$; the two independent row resamplings have already been absorbed into $T_r$. Negating every slice entry leaves $\bar A_{\underline y}$ unchanged and negates $\bar G_{\underline y}$. Their Fourier supports therefore have opposite total parities, so the cross terms vanish. The expected sign product is $\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(\bar A_{\underline y})-\mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t(\bar G_{\underline y})$. An edge is cut with probability one half of one minus its sign product, giving <a href="#eq:cut-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cut-stability">[eq:cut-stability]</a>. Nonnegativity of stability for $t>0$ gives <a href="#eq:cut-upper-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:cut-upper-stability">[eq:cut-upper-stability]</a>. ◻

</details>

If a cut has value at least $(1+B(t))/2+\delta/2$, then its odd average has expected slice stability at least $B(t)+\delta$. The next three sections show that this excess stability yields a label carrying substantial Fourier mass, with constants independent of the game dimension and the number of repetitions; the formal statement is [prop:extraction](#prop:extraction). [sec:decoding](#sec:decoding) then turns that mass into a labeling of a single copy of the original game.

<a id="sec:tree-fourier"></a>
### Fourier structure of the tree code

The cut depends on the generated word, so every transformation that preserves that word also constrains the cut’s row Fourier coefficients. At each gate these constraints give a common Fourier index shared by all child pairs. Negating the gate table distinguishes whether this index is an evaluation label. We then show that, at a random slice, little smoothed Fourier mass is lost by keeping the entire common index in just one child of each pair. Finally we express these retained indices in row coordinates that remain free after the other rows are fixed. This is the local form used in [sec:extraction](#sec:extraction). Throughout the section, $f$ is a function of the code in the sense of <a href="#eq:function-of-code" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:function-of-code">[eq:function-of-code]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-14"></a>
#### Row Fourier support

For each node $v$, define its *generator* $P(v)$, a set of leaves, recursively: $P(v)=\{v\}$ at a leaf, and $P(v)=P(v_{1,0})\cup P(v_{1,1})$ at an internal node. Thus a generator always follows both children of the first pair; unlike the slice, it involves no random choices. Adding the same $h\in H$ to all rows in $P(v)$ multiplies the subtree output at $a$ by $(-1)^{h(a)}$. This follows by induction from Equation <a href="#eq:gate-rule" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:gate-rule">[eq:gate-rule]</a>: multiplying both children of one pair by the same sign leaves all gate inputs $z_g$ unchanged and multiplies the prefactor by that sign.

<a id="eq:row-smoothing"></a>
For a row Fourier index $C=(q_i)_{i=1}^k\in(H^*)^k$, put $$\chi_C(R)=\prod_i\chi_{q_i}(R_i),\qquad
 |C|=|\{i:q_i\ne0\}|,\qquad
 q_v=\sum_{i\in P(v)}q_i.$$ Write $q_{v,gb}$ for the index of child $(g,b)$ of $v$. Thus, by definition, $q_v=q_{v,10}+q_{v,11}$. At fixed tables, the row expansion and its smoothing are $$f(R,F)=\sum_C\widehat f_C(F)\chi_C(R),\qquad
 \widehat{\bar f}_C(F)=r^{|C|}\widehat f_C(F).
 \label{eq:row-smoothing}$$

<a id="stmt-lem-row-symmetry"></a>
<div id="lem:row-symmetry" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:row-relations"></a>
<a id="eq:table-flip-action"></a>
<a id="eq:table-translation-action"></a>
**Lemma 9** (Row symmetries). *Let $f$ be a function of the code. Every nonzero row Fourier coefficient of $f$ satisfies, at each internal node $v$, $$q_{v,g0}+q_{v,g1}=q_v\qquad(g\in[s]).
 \label{eq:row-relations}$$ For a table collection $F$, let $F^{v,-}$ negate $F_v$ alone, and let $F^{v,z}$ replace $F_v(u)$ by $F_v(u+z)$, leaving all other tables unchanged. Then $$\begin{aligned}
 \widehat f_C(F^{v,-})
   &=(-1)^{q_v(\mathbf 1)}\widehat f_C(F),
   \label{eq:table-flip-action}\\
 \widehat f_C(F^{v,z})
   &=(-1)^{\sum_g z_g q_{v,g1}(\mathbf 1)}\widehat f_C(F).
   \label{eq:table-translation-action}
\end{aligned}$$ All these statements remain true after row smoothing. They are also preserved by row Fourier projections and by projection to any range of total Fourier degrees in the entries of a specified collection of whole gate tables.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-row-symmetry"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-row-symmetry</summary>

*Proof.* For each $g$, shifting the generators of both children in pair $g$ by $h$ has exactly the same effect on the subtree at $v$ as shifting $P(v)$ by $h$. Consequently the two row translations have the same effect on the whole word. Taking row Fourier coefficients yields $$\chi_{q_{v,g0}+q_{v,g1}}(h)\widehat f_C(F)
   =\chi_{q_v}(h)\widehat f_C(F)\qquad(h\in H).$$ Distinct characters are distinct functions, proving Equation <a href="#eq:row-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-relations">[eq:row-relations]</a>.

Negating $F_v$ has the same effect on the word as adding $\mathbf 1$ to all rows in $P(v)$. This proves Equation <a href="#eq:table-flip-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:table-flip-action">[eq:table-flip-action]</a>. To translate the input of $F_v$ by $z$, add $\mathbf 1$ to the generator of its second child in each pair with $z_g=1$. This changes precisely those gate input bits and leaves its prefactor unchanged. Fourier transformation gives Equation <a href="#eq:table-translation-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:table-translation-action">[eq:table-translation-action]</a>.

Row smoothing and row Fourier projections act diagonally on $C$, so preserve all three identities. On the cube of table-entry signs, a domain translation permutes coordinates, and negating a gate table multiplies each monomial by its parity sign. Both operations preserve total degree in any specified collection of whole gate tables and therefore commute with the stated degree projections. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-15"></a>
#### An unsplit projection on a random slice

Equation <a href="#eq:row-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-relations">[eq:row-relations]</a> assigns one common sum $q_v$ to every child pair. Equation <a href="#eq:table-flip-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:table-flip-action">[eq:table-flip-action]</a> then has a useful interpretation: coefficients odd under negating $F_v$ have $q_v(\mathbf 1)=1$, so their common index is a label by <a href="#eq:labels-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-dual">[eq:labels-dual]</a>. The individual child indices, however, can still be arbitrary functionals whose sum is $q_v$.

We simplify this last freedom at one gate at a time. Keeping $q_v$ in one child of each pair leaves only the choice of that child, rather than two arbitrary functionals with prescribed sum. Call $C$ *unsplit at $v$* if, for every $g$, the ordered pair $(q_{v,g0},q_{v,g1})$ is either $(q_v,0)$ or $(0,q_v)$. Let $S_v$ be the orthogonal row Fourier projection onto such indices. When $q_v=0$, this requires every child index to be zero. The next bound averages the cost over the slice depth. It does not require an index to be unsplit at every gate of the tree.

<a id="stmt-lem-unsplit"></a>
<div id="lem:unsplit" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:unsplit-bound"></a>
**Lemma 10** (Average cost of the unsplit projection). *Let $f$ be a function of the code with $|f|\le1$, let $r=1-\mu/d$ with $0<\mu<1/2$, and let $\bar f=T_rf$. Choose the random slice independently of $f$. Then $$\mathbb E_{J_0,\mathcal D}\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}
       \norm{(I-S_v)\bar f}_{2,R,F}^{2}
       \le \frac{s}{2e m\mu}.
 \label{eq:unsplit-bound}$$ The same bound holds after averaging over additional background data determining $f$, provided the slice is chosen independently of those data.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-unsplit"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-unsplit</summary>

*Proof.* Fix an index $C$ satisfying Equation <a href="#eq:row-relations" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-relations">[eq:row-relations]</a>, and let $N_j$ be the number of nodes at depth $j$ with nonzero index $q_v$. If $q_v\ne0$, each child pair contains at least one nonzero index. A failure of the unsplit condition at such a node supplies at least one additional nonzero child. If $q_v=0$, a failure supplies at least two nonzero children, whereas its minimum contribution is zero. Therefore the number $B_j$ of failures at depth $j$ satisfies $$B_j\le N_{j+1}-sN_j.$$ Using the slice inclusion probability and telescoping gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_{J_0,\mathcal D}\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}
          \mathbf 1_{\{C\text{ is split at }v\}}
 &\le \frac1m\sum_{j=0}^{m-1}
          \frac{N_{j+1}-sN_j}{s^j}\\
 &=\frac{s}{m}\left(\frac{N_m}{s^m}-N_0\right)
 \le\frac{s|C|}{md}.
\end{aligned}$$ Here $N_m=|C|$. By Equation <a href="#eq:row-smoothing" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:row-smoothing">[eq:row-smoothing]</a> and Parseval, the left side of Equation <a href="#eq:unsplit-bound" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:unsplit-bound">[eq:unsplit-bound]</a> is at most $$\frac{s}{m}\sum_C
   \left(r^{2|C|}\frac{|C|}{d}\right)
           \mathbb E_F|\widehat f_C(F)|^2.$$ For $x\ge0$, $xe^{-2\mu x}\le(2e\mu)^{-1}$, and $\log r\le-\mu/d$. Hence every parenthesized factor is at most $(2e\mu)^{-1}$, while $\sum_C\mathbb E_F|\widehat f_C(F)|^2=\norm f_2^2\le1$. This proves the bound. The proof is pointwise in the background data, so its average satisfies the same inequality. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-16"></a>
#### Free shifts and the remaining row coordinates

The extraction argument must use the common index after fixing most of the rows. We therefore choose coordinates in which translating a child generator changes one free row and leaves all other coordinates fixed. The unsplit projection will then act separately at each fixed context, and its retained characters will have an explicit form.

Fix a slice $\mathcal D$ and a node $v\in\mathcal D$. The $2s$ sets $P(v_{g,b})$ are nonempty and disjoint. Choose one representative leaf $i_{g,b}$ from each of them, using a rule depending only on the tree, and set $L_{g,b}=R_{i_{g,b}}$. For every other leaf $i$ in that generator, replace $R_i$ by $R_i+L_{g,b}$. Keep all rows outside these generators as they are. Denote the resulting $k-2s$ remaining coordinates by $\Gamma\in H^{k-2s}$.

<a id="stmt-lem-shift-coordinates"></a>
<div id="lem:shift-coordinates" class="lemma">

**Lemma 11** (Free-shift coordinates). *The change of coordinates $$H^k\longrightarrow H^{2s}\times H^{k-2s},\qquad
 R\longmapsto((L_{g,b}),\Gamma)$$ is a linear bijection. In particular, under uniform rows the free shifts are independent uniforms conditional on $\Gamma$. The Fourier index of $L_{g,b}$ corresponding to a full row index $C$ is $Q_{g,b}=q_{v,g b}$.*

*Consequently, at every fixed $\Gamma$, the operator $S_v$ acts as the Fourier projection in the $2s$ shifts onto precisely the unsplit shift indices. For a function of the code or its row smoothing, the shift coefficients vanish unless the sums $Q_{g,0}+Q_{g,1}$ are the same for every $g$. The table actions in Equations <a href="#eq:table-flip-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:table-flip-action">[eq:table-flip-action]</a> and <a href="#eq:table-translation-action" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:table-translation-action">[eq:table-translation-action]</a> hold for each shift coefficient at this fixed context, with $q_v=Q_{1,0}+Q_{1,1}$ and $q_{v,g1}=Q_{g,1}$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-shift-coordinates"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-shift-coordinates</summary>

*Proof.* The inverse map sets the representative row equal to $L_{g,b}$ and recovers every other row in its generator by adding $L_{g,b}$ to its recorded difference. This proves bijectivity. In a row character, the coefficient of $L_{g,b}$ is exactly $\sum_{i\in P(v_{g,b})}q_i=q_{v,g b}$. Thus the condition defining $S_v$ depends only on the shift index $Q$, not on any Fourier index of $\Gamma$. Grouping the full Fourier expansion by $Q$ proves its asserted action on each fixed fiber. The same grouping proves the support and table-action identities: all full row coefficients with a given $Q$ satisfy the same identities and carry the same signs. ◻

</details>

Thus, for a nonzero common index $q$, the retained shift indices are specified by the subset $I\subseteq[s]$ of pairs in which $q$ occurs in the second child. Their characters are $$\prod_{g\notin I}\chi_q(L_{g0})
 \prod_{g\in I}\chi_q(L_{g1}).$$ If $q=0$, there is only the all-zero unsplit index, with character $1$. Negating $F_v$ multiplies the corresponding coefficient by $(-1)^{q(\mathbf 1)}$. On the part odd in $F_v$, $q$ is therefore a label and hence nonzero, so the description by $q$ and $I$ is unique. For such a label, translating the argument of $F_v$ by $z\in\mathbb F_2^s$ multiplies its coefficient by $(-1)^{\sum_{g\in I}z_g}$. Thus the same subset $I$ records both the row character and the table translation character. This is the local Fourier description used in [sec:extraction](#sec:extraction), valid at each fixed context.

<a id="sec:hashing"></a>
### A vector-valued hashing lemma

The following lemma turns dispersion among characters into dispersion among table entries. Its constants are independent of the dimension of the character space and of the dimension of the vectors being hashed. In the application, $q$ will index candidate labels and $z$ will index entries of a gate table. The resulting hashed vector will collect the Fourier coefficients contributing to one entry’s influence, so its squared norm will be that influence. This is why we need a vector-valued statement rather than a scalar estimate.

<a id="stmt-lem-hashing"></a>
<div id="lem:hashing" class="lemma">

<a id="eq:hashing-total-mass"></a>
<a id="eq:hashing-dispersion"></a>
**Lemma 12** (Vector-valued hashing). *Let $H$ be a finite-dimensional vector space over $\mathbb F_2$, write $\chi_q(u)=(-1)^{q(u)}$ for $q\in H^*$ and $u\in H$, let $\mathcal V$ be a real Hilbert space, let $s\ge1$ be an integer, and let $$c(q,z)\in\mathcal V,
 \qquad q\in H^*,\quad z\in\mathbb F_2^s.$$ Set $$w_q=\sum_{z\in\mathbb F_2^s}\norm{c(q,z)}^2,
 \qquad M=\sum_{q\in H^*}w_q.$$ Choose $L,J_1,\ldots,J_s$ independently and uniformly in $H$, and define $$\mathbf J(q)=(q(J_1),\ldots,q(J_s)),
 \qquad
 U(z)=\sum_{q\in H^*}\chi_q(L)c(q,z+\mathbf J(q)).$$ Then $$\label{eq:hashing-total-mass}
 \mathbb E\sum_{z\in\mathbb F_2^s}\norm{U(z)}^2=M.$$ For every $\zeta>0$, there are $\gamma>0$ and $s_0\in\mathbb N$, depending only on $\zeta$, such that for every $s\ge s_0$, $$\label{eq:hashing-dispersion}
 \max_{q\in H^*}w_q\le\gamma M
 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad
 \mathbb E\max_{z\in\mathbb F_2^s}\norm{U(z)}^2\le\zeta M.$$*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-hashing"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-hashing</summary>

*Proof.* All expectations over $H$ and $\mathbb F_2^s$ below use uniform probability measure; the displayed sums defining $w_q$ and $M$ are unnormalized. Conditional on the $J_g$, orthogonality of the characters of $L$ gives $$\mathbb E_L\norm{U(z)}^2
 =\sum_q\norm{c(q,z+\mathbf J(q))}^2.$$ Summing over $z$ proves <a href="#eq:hashing-total-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-total-mass">[eq:hashing-total-mass]</a>. If $M=0$, every vector $c(q,z)$ is zero and <a href="#eq:hashing-dispersion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-dispersion">[eq:hashing-dispersion]</a> is immediate. Henceforth assume $M>0$.

<a id="eq:hashing-quantitative"></a>
Set $h=2^s$ and $\rho=\max_q w_q/M$. We will prove the quantitative bound below for every $C,D>0$, and then choose its parameters. The main estimate bounds the maximum squared norm by the square root of the sum of fourth powers. After a Fourier change of variables, most terms in that fourth moment force the same character $q$ in all four factors; their total is controlled by the dispersion of the character masses. Two truncations will control the remaining terms and the cost of this change to the array: $$\label{eq:hashing-quantitative}
 \mathbb E\max_z\norm{U(z)}^2
 \le 2M\left[
 \sqrt{2\rho+\frac{2C^2}{D^2}+\frac{3D^4}{h}}
 +\left(\frac1{\sqrt C}+\frac1D\right)^2+\frac1h
 \right].$$

<a id="pm-h-18"></a>
###### Fourier representation.

<a id="eq:hashing-inversion"></a>
For $p\in\mathbb F_2^s$ and $u\in H$, define $$\alpha_q(p)=\sum_{z\in\mathbb F_2^s}(-1)^{p\cdot z}c(q,z),
 \qquad
 A_p(u)=\sum_{q\in H^*}\chi_q(u)\alpha_q(p),
 \qquad
 S_p=L+\sum_{g=1}^s p_gJ_g.$$ Fourier inversion in $z$ gives $$\label{eq:hashing-inversion}
 U(z)=\mathbb E_p(-1)^{p\cdot z}A_p(S_p).$$ Writing $m_p=\mathbb E_u\norm{A_p(u)}^2$, Parseval’s identity in the two groups gives <a id="eq:hashing-parseval"></a>$$\label{eq:hashing-parseval}
 m_p=\sum_q\norm{\alpha_q(p)}^2,
 \qquad \mathbb E_p m_p=M,
 \qquad \mathbb E_p\norm{\alpha_q(p)}^2=w_q.$$ For distinct $p,p'$, the variables $S_p,S_{p'}$ are independent and uniform in $H$: the coefficient vectors $(1,p),(1,p')$ are linearly independent over $\mathbb F_2$. Similarly, any three distinct $p_1,p_2,p_3$ give three independent uniform variables $S_{p_1},S_{p_2},S_{p_3}$. Indeed, any nonempty subcollection of their coefficient vectors whose sum is zero must have even cardinality, and hence would consist of two equal vectors.

<a id="pm-h-19"></a>
###### Two truncations.

<a id="eq:hashing-truncated-mass"></a>
We first discard indices $p$ with unusually large mean squared norm $m_p$, and then discard unusually large values of $A_p(u)$ on the remaining indices. The first cutoff makes the change to each individual character coefficient uniformly small under the second cutoff. The second gives the pointwise bound needed when the fourth-moment indices coincide. We will control the discarded part using a first-moment bound and a variance bound from the pairwise independence of the samples $S_p$. Define $$A'_p(u)=
 \begin{cases}
 A_p(u),&m_p\le CM\text{ and }\norm{A_p(u)}\le D\sqrt M,\\
 0,&\text{otherwise},
 \end{cases}$$ and put $$\alpha'_q(p)=\mathbb E_u\chi_q(u)A'_p(u),
 \qquad w'_q=\mathbb E_p\norm{\alpha'_q(p)}^2,
 \qquad U'(z)=\mathbb E_p(-1)^{p\cdot z}A'_p(S_p).$$ Pointwise truncation does not increase the total squared norm, so Parseval gives $\sum_qw'_q\le M$. If $m_p\le CM$, then $$\norm{\alpha_q(p)-\alpha'_q(p)}
 \le\mathbb E_u\norm{A_p(u)}
       \mathbf 1_{\{\norm{A_p(u)}>D\sqrt M\}}
 \le\frac{m_p}{D\sqrt M}
 \le\frac{C\sqrt M}{D}.$$ If $m_p>CM$, then $\alpha'_q(p)=0$. Consequently, $$\label{eq:hashing-truncated-mass}
 \sum_qw'_q\le M,
 \qquad
 w'_q\le 2w_q+\frac{2C^2M}{D^2}.$$

<a id="eq:hashing-truncation-error"></a>
Let $$X_p=\norm{(A_p-A'_p)(S_p)},\qquad X=\mathbb E_pX_p.$$ Equation <a href="#eq:hashing-inversion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-inversion">[eq:hashing-inversion]</a> and the triangle inequality imply $\max_z\norm{U(z)-U'(z)}\le X$. The contribution to $\mathbb EX$ from the indices with $m_p>CM$ is at most $$\mathbb E_p\mathbf 1_{\{m_p>CM\}}\sqrt{m_p}
 \le\frac{\mathbb E_p m_p}{\sqrt{CM}}=\sqrt{M/C}.$$ The contribution from the remaining indices is at most $\mathbb E_p m_p/(D\sqrt M)=\sqrt M/D$. Moreover, the $X_p$ are pairwise independent, by the pairwise independence of the $S_p$. Since $A_p-A'_p$ is either $A_p$ or zero at each point, $$\operatorname{Var}(X)
 =h^{-2}\sum_p\operatorname{Var}(X_p)
 \le h^{-2}\sum_p\mathbb E_u\norm{A_p(u)-A'_p(u)}^2
 \le\frac Mh.$$ We have proved $$\label{eq:hashing-truncation-error}
 \mathbb E\max_z\norm{U(z)-U'(z)}^2
 \le M\left[\left(\frac1{\sqrt C}+\frac1D\right)^2+\frac1h\right].$$

<a id="pm-h-20"></a>
###### The fourth moment.

<a id="eq:hashing-fourth-expansion"></a>
For brevity write $B_p=A'_p(S_p)$. Expanding the fourth power of the norm and summing the characters in $z$ gives the exact identity $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\sum_z\norm{U'(z)}^4
 &=h^{-3}\sum_{p_1,p_2,p_3}
   \mathbb E\bigl[\langle B_{p_1},B_{p_2}\rangle
                  \langle B_{p_3},B_{p_1+p_2+p_3}\rangle\bigr]\notag\\
 &=\mathbb E_{p_1,p_2,p_3}\mathbb E
   \bigl[\langle B_{p_1},B_{p_2}\rangle\langle B_{p_3},B_{p_4}\rangle\bigr],
 \qquad p_4=p_1+p_2+p_3.
 \label{eq:hashing-fourth-expansion}
\end{aligned}$$ Here $p_1,p_2,p_3$ are independent uniform indices in the second line. The factor $h^{-3}$ in the first line comes from four factors $h^{-1}$ in Fourier inversion and the sum of $h$ characters over $z$.

If any two of $p_1,p_2,p_3,p_4$ coincide, then some two of $p_1,p_2,p_3$ coincide. This event has probability at most $3/h$. Since $\norm{B_p}\le D\sqrt M$, its contribution to <a href="#eq:hashing-fourth-expansion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-fourth-expansion">[eq:hashing-fourth-expansion]</a> is at most $3D^4M^2/h$ in absolute value.

<a id="eq:hashing-common-character"></a>
Now fix four distinct indices with $p_4=p_1+p_2+p_3$. The first three $S_{p_i}$ are independent uniform elements of $H$, and $S_{p_4}=S_{p_1}+S_{p_2}+S_{p_3}$. Expanding the four $A'_{p_i}$ in characters and averaging over those three independent elements yields $$\label{eq:hashing-common-character}
 \mathbb E\bigl[\langle B_{p_1},B_{p_2}\rangle\langle B_{p_3},B_{p_4}\rangle\bigr]
 =\sum_q
   \langle \alpha'_q(p_1),\alpha'_q(p_2)\rangle
   \langle \alpha'_q(p_3),\alpha'_q(p_4)\rangle.$$ In detail, characters indexed by $q_1,q_2,q_3,q_4$ produce the factor $$\prod_{i=1}^3\chi_{q_i+q_4}(S_{p_i}),$$ whose expectation is zero unless $q_1=q_2=q_3=q_4$.

<a id="eq:hashing-truncated-maximum"></a>
The right side of <a href="#eq:hashing-common-character" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-common-character">[eq:hashing-common-character]</a> need not be nonnegative. For an upper bound, replace each summand by $\prod_{i=1}^4\norm{\alpha'_q(p_i)}$, which is nonnegative, and then drop the restriction that the four indices be distinct. For every fixed $q$, Cauchy–Schwarz gives $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E_{p_1,p_2,p_3}\prod_{i=1}^4\norm{\alpha'_q(p_i)}
 &\le
 \left(\mathbb E\norm{\alpha'_q(p_1)}^2
             \norm{\alpha'_q(p_2)}^2\right)^{1/2}
 \left(\mathbb E\norm{\alpha'_q(p_3)}^2
             \norm{\alpha'_q(p_4)}^2\right)^{1/2}\\
 &=(w'_q)^2.
\end{aligned}$$ The last equality uses the unconditioned independent uniform law of each pair $(p_1,p_2)$ and $(p_3,p_4)$; it does not assert independence after conditioning on distinctness. Thus the nondegenerate fourth moment is bounded by $\sum_q(w'_q)^2$: dispersion among characters now controls concentration among table entries. Combining these estimates with <a href="#eq:hashing-truncated-mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-truncated-mass">[eq:hashing-truncated-mass]</a> proves $$\begin{aligned}
 \mathbb E\sum_z\norm{U'(z)}^4
 &\le \sum_q(w'_q)^2+\frac{3D^4M^2}{h}\\
 &\le M^2\left(2\rho+\frac{2C^2}{D^2}+\frac{3D^4}{h}\right).
\end{aligned}$$ Therefore, using Cauchy–Schwarz once more, $$\label{eq:hashing-truncated-maximum}
 \mathbb E\max_z\norm{U'(z)}^2
 \le\left(\mathbb E\sum_z\norm{U'(z)}^4\right)^{1/2}
 \le M\sqrt{2\rho+\frac{2C^2}{D^2}+\frac{3D^4}{h}}.$$ The inequality $(a+b)^2\le2a^2+2b^2$, together with <a href="#eq:hashing-truncation-error" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-truncation-error">[eq:hashing-truncation-error]</a> and <a href="#eq:hashing-truncated-maximum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-truncated-maximum">[eq:hashing-truncated-maximum]</a>, proves <a href="#eq:hashing-quantitative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-quantitative">[eq:hashing-quantitative]</a>.

Finally, set $\varepsilon=\min\{\zeta,1\}$ and choose $$C=\frac{64}{\varepsilon},\qquad
 D=\frac{8C}{\varepsilon},\qquad
 \gamma=\frac{\varepsilon^2}{128},\qquad
 2^{s_0}\ge
 \max\left\{\frac{192D^4}{\varepsilon^2},\frac{16}{\varepsilon}\right\}.$$ If $s\ge s_0$ and $\rho\le\gamma$, the square-root term in <a href="#eq:hashing-quantitative" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-quantitative">[eq:hashing-quantitative]</a> is at most $\varepsilon/4$. Also $D\ge\sqrt C$, so the other two terms in its brackets are at most $\varepsilon/16$ each. The resulting bound is $3\varepsilon M/4\le\zeta M$, establishing <a href="#eq:hashing-dispersion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:hashing-dispersion">[eq:hashing-dispersion]</a> with constants depending only on $\zeta$. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:extraction"></a>
### From excess stability to a label

This section contains the analytic statement used in the reduction. Its constants do not depend on the dimension of the affine label space. The functions may depend on an additional random parameter $\omega$, whose law is independent of all rows, gate tables, and the random slice. In the application, $\omega$ will be a tuple of right endpoints of the game.

Let $\phi_\omega:\{-1,1\}^{\mathcal A}\to[-1,1]$ be odd, meaning $\phi_\omega(-w)=-\phi_\omega(w)$, and set $$f_\omega(R,F)=\phi_\omega(W(R,F)),\qquad
 \bar f_\omega=T_r f_\omega,\qquad r=1-\mu/d.$$ The word, tree, and random slice $\mathcal D$ are those of [sec:tree](#sec:tree). Write $F_{\mathrm{out}}$ for the tables outside $\mathcal D$. Once $\omega,\mathcal D,R,F_{\mathrm{out}}$ are fixed, $\bar f_\omega$ is a function of the independent slice-table bits.

<a id="eq:mass"></a>
For a node $v\in\mathcal D$, use the free shift coordinates $(L_{gb})_{g\in[s],b\in\{0,1\}}$ and the remaining context $\Gamma\in H^{k-2s}$ from [lem:shift-coordinates](#lem:shift-coordinates). A hat marked “shifts” expands only these $2s$ free rows. For a label $q\in\mathcal A$ (equivalently, $q\in H^*$ with $q(\mathbf 1)=1$), define $$\label{eq:mass}
 m_q(f;\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}})
 =\mathbb E_{F_{\mathcal D}}
 \sum_{\substack{Q\in(H^*)^{2s}\\
                  Q_{g0}+Q_{g1}=q\ (g\in[s])}}
 \abs{\widehat f^{\,\mathrm{shifts}}(Q)}^2.$$ The dependence on $v,\mathcal D$, and $\omega$ is suppressed. The sets of indices in this definition are disjoint as $q$ varies. For a fixed $q$, the mass includes every way to distribute that common sum between the two children of each pair. We seek such mass for the original smoothed function $\bar f_\omega$. The unsplit projection and a table-degree cutoff will serve only to find a label carrying this mass; neither projection is part of the conclusion. Keeping all the indices with a given common sum will let us compare masses under affine pullback in [sec:decoding](#sec:decoding).

In particular, if $|f|\le1$, then at every fixed context <a id="eq:mass-bound"></a>$$\label{eq:mass-bound}
 0\le\sum_{q\in\mathcal A}m_q(f;\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}})\le1.$$

<a id="stmt-prop-extraction"></a>
<div id="prop:extraction" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:excess-stability"></a>
<a id="eq:extraction-event"></a>
**Proposition 13** (Dimension-independent extraction). *Fix $t\in(0,1)$, $0<\delta<1-B(t)$, and $0<\mu<1/2$. There are tree parameters $s\ge2,m\ge1$ and constants $p_*,\theta>0$, depending only on $t,\delta,\mu$, with the following property. For any affine label space $\mathcal A$ and any family $\phi_\omega$ as above, if $$\label{eq:excess-stability}
 \mathbb E_{\omega,\mathcal D,R,F_{\mathrm{out}}}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Stab}}_t^{F_{\mathcal D}}(\bar f_\omega)
 \ge B(t)+\delta,$$ then $$\label{eq:extraction-event}
 \Pr\left[
   \max_{q\in\mathcal A}
   m_q(\bar f_\omega;\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}})\ge\theta
 \right]\ge p_*.$$ Here we sample $\omega$, the random slice $\mathcal D$, and a uniform node $v\in\mathcal D$; conditional on these, we sample $F_{\mathrm{out}}$ and $\Gamma$ independently and uniformly.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-prop-extraction"><summary>Proof of stmt-prop-extraction</summary>

*Proof.* Excess stability first produces an influential slice entry. We retain that influence after the unsplit projection, discard the part even in its gate table, and express the remaining influence as an affine hash of vectors indexed by labels. The hashing lemma then forces one label to carry substantial conditional mass.

All constants chosen below are independent of $\mathcal A$ and of the law of $\omega$. The degree cutoff $K$ and influence threshold $\tau$ are chosen first. We leave $s,m$ unspecified during the estimates: Step 4 will choose $s$ to control the even and hashed contributions, and then $m$ to control the cost of the unsplit projection.

<a id="pm-h-22"></a>
###### Step 1: find an influential slice entry.

Simultaneously negating the slice tables negates the word. Since $\phi_\omega$ is odd and smoothing preserves this identity, $\bar f_\omega$ has mean zero in the slice bits for every fixed $\omega,\mathcal D,R,F_{\mathrm{out}}$. It also lies in $[-1,1]$. Apply [lem:mis](#lem:mis) with $\xi=\delta/2$, obtaining $K\ge1$ and $\tau>0$. We may decrease $\tau$ to ensure $\tau\le1$. Since stability is at most one, <a href="#eq:excess-stability" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:excess-stability">[eq:excess-stability]</a> implies that, with probability at least $$\eta=\delta/2,$$ some entry $z\in\mathbb F_2^s$ of some $v\in\mathcal D$ has $\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}^{\le K}(\bar f_\omega)\ge\tau$. Indeed, if the probability of this event is $p$, the expected stability is at most $B(t)+\delta/2+p$.

<a id="eq:choose-m"></a>
<a id="eq:large-max-sum"></a>
Let $S_v$ be the orthogonal row projection onto indices unsplit at $v$. Put $$H_v=P_{\le K}^{F_{\mathcal D}}S_v\bar f_\omega.$$ By [lem:unsplit](#lem:unsplit), the required bound on the projection loss is $$\label{eq:choose-m}
 \mathbb E\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}
 \norm{(1-S_v)\bar f_\omega}_{2,R,F}^2
 \le\frac{s}{2em\mu}\le\frac{\eta\tau}{8}.$$ For any fixed $s$, the last inequality can be ensured by increasing $m$. The same expectation is the expectation over $\omega,\mathcal D,R,F_{\mathrm{out}}$ of the sum of the corresponding conditional squared norms in $F_{\mathcal D}$. Markov’s inequality shows that this sum is at most $\tau/4$ except on an event of probability $\eta/2$. On the intersection with the influential-entry event, the triangle inequality for the projection defining that influence gives $$\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v)}
 \ge\sqrt{\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}^{\le K}(\bar f_\omega)}
      -\norm{(1-S_v)\bar f_\omega}_{2,F_{\mathcal D}}
 \ge\frac{\sqrt\tau}{2}.$$ Consequently, $$\label{eq:large-max-sum}
 \mathbb E\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}\max_z\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v)
 \ge\frac{\eta\tau}{8}=:\Lambda.$$

<a id="eq:total-influence"></a>
We will also use $$\label{eq:total-influence}
 \mathbb E\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}\sum_z\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v)\le K.$$ To verify it, integrate over all rows. Each $S_v$ is an orthogonal projection in the rows and commutes with the slice-bit Fourier projections. It can therefore only decrease the integrated influence at its entries. After dropping each $S_v$ separately, the left side is bounded by the total influence of $P_{\le K}^{F_{\mathcal D}}\bar f_\omega$, averaged over the remaining variables. Equation <a href="#eq:degree-influence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:degree-influence">[eq:degree-influence]</a> and $|\bar f_\omega|\le1$ give <a href="#eq:total-influence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-influence">[eq:total-influence]</a>. No pointwise boundedness of $H_v$ is asserted or needed.

<a id="pm-h-23"></a>
###### Step 2: discard even dependence on a gate.

<a id="eq:even-influence"></a>
Fix $v\in\mathcal D$, and split $H_v$ into its even and odd parts under negation of $F_v$ alone. These parts have disjoint Fourier supports in the slice bits, also after retaining the monomials containing any specified entry. Thus $$\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v)=
 \mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v^{\mathrm{even}})
 +\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v^{\mathrm{odd}}).$$ By the sign action in [lem:row-symmetry](#lem:row-symmetry), the even part has $q_v(\mathbf 1)=0$ on row support. Since the index is unsplit, all its child indices are either $0$ or $q_v$, so they too take value zero on $\mathbf 1$. The domain-translation action in that lemma shows that the even part is invariant under translations of the domain of $F_v$, with the rows and outside tables fixed. These translations act transitively on its entries. All entry influences at $v$ are therefore equal. By <a href="#eq:total-influence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:total-influence">[eq:total-influence]</a>, $$\label{eq:even-influence}
 \mathbb E\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}
 \max_z\mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v^{\mathrm{even}})\le\frac{K}{2^s}.$$

<a id="pm-h-24"></a>
###### Step 3: express odd influences as affine hashes.

Fix $\omega,\mathcal D,v,\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}}$. The remaining row coordinates $L_{gb}$ are independent uniform elements of $H$. On the odd part, the common pair sum $q$ is a label, by <a href="#eq:labels-dual" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:labels-dual">[eq:labels-dual]</a>, and hence is nonzero. An unsplit shift index with common sum $q$ puts $q$ in the second child on a subset $I\subseteq[s]$ and in the first child elsewhere. Let $a_{q,I}(F_{\mathcal D})$ be its coefficient in $H_v^{\mathrm{odd}}$.

<a id="eq:odd-hash-form"></a>
Set $$L_\Sigma=\sum_{g=1}^sL_{g0},\qquad J_g=L_{g0}+L_{g1},\qquad
 \mathbf J(q)=(q(J_1),\ldots,q(J_s)).$$ The $s+1$ forms $L_\Sigma,J_1,\ldots,J_s$ are independent and uniform: the displayed linear map from $H^{2s}$ onto $H^{s+1}$ is surjective. The remaining $s-1$ coordinates do not occur in the unsplit expansion. The character of the shift index $(q,I)$ is $$\chi_q(L_\Sigma)(-1)^{\sum_{g\in I}q(J_g)}.$$ On the other hand, the domain-translation identity gives $$a_{q,I}(F_v(\cdot+z),F_{\mathcal D\setminus\{v\}})
 =(-1)^{\sum_{g\in I}z_g}a_{q,I}(F_{\mathcal D}).$$ With $\Psi_q=\sum_{I\subseteq[s]}a_{q,I}$ we obtain $$\label{eq:odd-hash-form}
 H_v^{\mathrm{odd}}
 =\sum_{q\in\mathcal A}\chi_q(L_\Sigma)
 \Psi_q\bigl(F_v(\cdot+\mathbf J(q)),
                   F_{\mathcal D\setminus\{v\}}\bigr).$$ The functions $a_{q,I}$ for distinct $I$ are orthogonal in the uniform slice bits: a domain translation whose two characters differ preserves their inner product and changes its sign. Consequently, <a id="eq:psi-mass"></a>$$\label{eq:psi-mass}
 \norm{\Psi_q}_{2,F_{\mathcal D}}^2
 =\sum_I\norm{a_{q,I}}_{2,F_{\mathcal D}}^2
 \le m_q(\bar f_\omega;\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}}).$$ The inequality follows because the unsplit row projection is determined by the free shift index, and the degree and parity projections are orthogonal projections in the slice bits at each fixed context. Every $\Psi_q$ has degree at most $K$.

Equation <a href="#eq:odd-hash-form" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:odd-hash-form">[eq:odd-hash-form]</a> has converted the free-shift characters into translations of a gate table. We next collect its Boolean Fourier coefficients into vectors whose squared norms are entry influences. These Fourier coefficients are indexed by sets of table entries; the earlier index $I\subseteq[s]$ instead specified a character of the table’s domain $\mathbb F_2^s$.

<a id="eq:influence-hash"></a>
<a id="eq:wq-mq"></a>
<a id="eq:sum-M"></a>
Denote by $\widehat\Psi_q(B,D)$ the slice Fourier coefficient with entry set $B\subseteq\mathbb F_2^s$ at gate $v$ and entry set $D$ on the other slice gates. For $z\in\mathbb F_2^s$ define a real vector $c(q,z)$, with coordinates indexed by pairs $(A,D)$ where $0\in A\subseteq\mathbb F_2^s$, by $$c(q,z)_{A,D}=\widehat\Psi_q(z+A,D).$$ Using entry sets relative to $z$ makes this vector space the same for every $z$ and every label $q$. Set $c(q,z)=0$ for functionals $q$ which are not labels. For the influence at $z$, a slice Fourier set $B$ containing $z$ has the unique form $B=z+A$ with $0\in A$. Translation by $\mathbf J(q)$ sends its coefficient to $\widehat\Psi_q(z+A+\mathbf J(q),D)$. Hence, exactly, $$\label{eq:influence-hash}
 \mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v^{\mathrm{odd}})
 =\norm*{\sum_q\chi_q(L_\Sigma)c(q,z+\mathbf J(q))}^2.$$ Writing $w_q=\sum_z\norm{c(q,z)}^2$ and $M_v=\sum_qw_q$, we have $$\label{eq:wq-mq}
 w_q=\sum_{B,D}|B|\abs{\widehat\Psi_q(B,D)}^2
 \le K\norm{\Psi_q}_2^2
 \le K m_q(\bar f_\omega;\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}}).$$ The factor $|B|$ counts the entries whose influences contain that Fourier monomial; it is at most the monomial’s total degree, which is at most $K$. Thus $M_v\le K$ at every context. The total-mass identity in [lem:hashing](#lem:hashing), together with <a href="#eq:influence-hash" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:influence-hash">[eq:influence-hash]</a>, also gives $$\label{eq:sum-M}
 \mathbb E\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}M_v
 =\mathbb E\sum_{v\in\mathcal D}\sum_z
     \mathop{\mathrm{Inf}}_{v,z}(H_v^{\mathrm{odd}})\le K.$$ For this identity the free coordinates are integrated separately for each node; their different coordinate systems do not affect the uniform row law.

<a id="pm-h-25"></a>
###### Step 4: force a heavy label.

Choose $\zeta>0$ with $\zeta K\le\Lambda/4$. Apply [lem:hashing](#lem:hashing), obtaining $\gamma>0$ and a lower bound on $s$. Choose $s\ge2$ large enough for that lemma and for $K2^{-s}\le\Lambda/4$. Then choose $m$ as in <a href="#eq:choose-m" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:choose-m">[eq:choose-m]</a>. Finally put <a id="eq:extraction-constants"></a>$$\label{eq:extraction-constants}
 M_0=\frac{\Lambda}{4\cdot2^m},\qquad
 \theta=\frac{\gamma M_0}{K},\qquad
 p_* =\frac{\Lambda}{4K\,2^m}.$$

For each node, condition on $\omega,\mathcal D,v,\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}}$ and integrate the free shifts. Partition these contexts into three classes:

1.  $w_q\le\gamma M_v$ for every $q$;

2.  the first condition fails and $M_v<M_0$;

3.  $M_v\ge M_0$ and $w_q>\gamma M_v$ for some $q$.

On class (i), the hashing lemma bounds the expected maximum odd influence by $\zeta M_v$. Summing and using <a href="#eq:sum-M" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:sum-M">[eq:sum-M]</a> costs at most $\Lambda/4$. On class (ii), the maximum is bounded in expectation by the total influence, namely $M_v<M_0$. Since $|\mathcal D|\le2^m$, these contexts cost at most $\Lambda/4$. On class (iii), the same total-mass bound is at most $K$ per node. Equation <a href="#eq:even-influence" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:even-influence">[eq:even-influence]</a> bounds all even contributions by $\Lambda/4$. Let $N_{\mathrm{iii}}$ count the nodes whose induced contexts lie in class (iii). Comparing all four contributions with <a href="#eq:large-max-sum" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:large-max-sum">[eq:large-max-sum]</a> gives $$\Lambda
 \le K2^{-s}+\zeta K+2^m M_0+K\mathbb EN_{\mathrm{iii}}
 \le\frac{3\Lambda}{4}+K\mathbb EN_{\mathrm{iii}}.$$ Thus $\mathbb EN_{\mathrm{iii}}\ge\Lambda/(4K)$. At each such node <a href="#eq:wq-mq" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:wq-mq">[eq:wq-mq]</a> supplies a label with $m_q>\gamma M_0/K=\theta$. Choosing a uniform node from the slice loses at most a factor $2^m$ and gives <a href="#eq:extraction-event" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:extraction-event">[eq:extraction-event]</a>. This proves the proposition. ◻

</details>

<a id="sec:decoding"></a>
### Decoding with a hidden coordinate

We now convert the Fourier mass supplied by Proposition <a href="#prop:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:extraction">13</a> into a labeling of the original affine game. The difficulty is that the mass is measured after fixing a row context. A labeler at an $X$-vertex does not know the projection on its incident edge, and therefore cannot usually lift that context to its own label space. We resolve this by repeating the game coordinates and planting one coordinate on which the context and the latent resampling rows have zero linear coefficients. Hiding the planted coordinate makes the resulting distribution close to the original one. The comparison across the unknown projection then involves only the $2s$ free shifts, so its loss is independent of $n$ and $T$.

Throughout this section, the cut tables are fixed. We use the tree parameters and the row coordinates of the preceding sections. For each choice of a slice $\mathcal D$ and a node $v\in\mathcal D$, fix the coordinate change $$R\longleftrightarrow (L,\Gamma),\qquad
 L=(L_{gb})_{g\in[s],\,b\in\{0,1\}}\in H^{2s},
 \quad \Gamma\in H^{k-2s},$$ by choosing its representative leaves in a deterministic order. This change uses only additions of row coordinates and depends only on the tree and $v$. In particular, it commutes with pullback of affine forms. The masses $m_q$ always refer to these coordinates and to the uniform average over the slice tables in Equation <a href="#eq:mass" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:mass">[eq:mass]</a>.

<a id="pm-h-27"></a>
#### Removing the row smoothing

Let $R^{\mathrm{new}}_1,\ldots,R^{\mathrm{new}}_k$ be independent uniform elements of $H$, and independently let $B_1,\ldots,B_k$ be Bernoulli variables with $\Pr(B_i=1)=1-r$. Set $$E_i=B_iR^{\mathrm{new}}_i,
 \qquad E=(E_1,\ldots,E_k).$$ For any fixed row array $R$, the array $R+E$ has the distribution of the one-copy row resampling used by the test. Write $(E_L,E_\Gamma)$ for the coordinates of $E$ under the above linear change of variables.

<a id="stmt-lem-unsmooth"></a>
<div id="lem:unsmooth" class="lemma">

**Lemma 14** (Removing smoothing). *For every fixed $\underline y$, $\mathcal D$, $v$, $\Gamma$, $F_{\mathrm{out}}$, and label $q\in\mathcal A$, <a id="eq:unsmooth"></a>$$\label{eq:unsmooth}
 m_q(\bar G_{\underline y};\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}})
 \leq
 \mathbb E_{E,\,\underline e\mid\underline y}
 m_q(G_{\underline e};\Gamma+E_\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}}).$$ Here the noise $E$ is independent of the conditionally sampled edge tuple $\underline e$.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-lem-unsmooth"><summary>Proof of stmt-lem-unsmooth</summary>

*Proof.* For the fixed context, regard a function of $(L,F_{\mathcal D})$ as an element of the real Hilbert space with the uniform probability measure. Let $\Pi_q$ be the orthogonal projection onto the shift Fourier indices $Q$ satisfying $Q_{g0}+Q_{g1}=q$ for every $g$. Then the corresponding mass is the squared norm of this projection. The identity $$\bar G_{\underline y}(L,\Gamma,F)
 =\mathbb E_{E,\,\underline e\mid\underline y}
 G_{\underline e}(L+E_L,\Gamma+E_\Gamma,F)$$ and convexity of the squared Hilbert-space norm give the desired inequality. Indeed, translation by $E_L$ commutes with $\Pi_q$ and preserves its norm: on each shift Fourier coefficient it only multiplies by a sign. ◻

</details>

<a id="eq:decoder-choice"></a>
Choose once and for all an ordering of each label space, and define $$\label{eq:decoder-choice}
 q^*=q^*(\underline y,\mathcal D,v,\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}})
 \in\operatorname*{arg\,max}_{q\in\mathcal A}
 m_q(\bar G_{\underline y};\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}})$$ using that ordering to break ties. This definition uses the original conditional edge average and the original row-smoothing operator. It will remain unchanged when we modify the distribution of the row data below.

<a id="eq:unplanted-score"></a>
Sample $\underline y$, the slice, its uniform node, the context, and the outside tables as in Proposition <a href="#prop:extraction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:extraction">13</a>, and then sample $R^{\mathrm{new}}$, $B$, and $\underline e\mid\underline y$. Combining that Proposition with Lemma <a href="#lem:unsmooth" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="lem:unsmooth">14</a> gives $$\label{eq:unplanted-score}
 \mathbb E\,m_{q^*}(G_{\underline e};
              \Gamma+E_\Gamma,F_{\mathrm{out}})
